I even got close enough to see its little feathers when it stopped to rest on a sign. \n\nSo, yeah, birds don't bother me one bit. Same with pigeons in the parking lot, or seagulls. I went to Ocean City a few years back and those gulls are huge, they'll swoop right down and steal a french fry right out of your hand if you're not paying attention. Annoying? Sure. But scary? Not at all. It's just... it doesn't trigger that alarm bell in my head. If that sparrow in Aisle 4 had been a bat, though? I would have left my shift right then and there. I would have been halfway down the highway before my manager even knew I was gone. So it really is just the bats, nothing else with wings does that to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had this intense fear reaction when seeing a picture or a video of a bat, rather than a real one?\n\nPatient: Pictures or videos? Oh, man, yeah. It's... it's actually ridiculous how bad it is. Like, around Halloween, right? Working in retail, you know how it is, we start putting out the seasonal stuff in, like, late August. And there are always these cardboard cutouts, or those motion-activated props, or even just the graphics on the big bags of candy. My manager, Greg, he knows I hate them, but corporate sends what corporate sends, so we have to put them on the endcaps. And even just seeing a picture of one on the packaging... I have to look away. I get this awful tightness in my chest and my hands get all clammy. \n\nOr if I'm just sitting at home, scrolling on my phone, you know, watching some random video on social media or whatever, and suddenly a clip from a nature documentary pops up on the screen? I've literally thrown my phone across the couch. My girlfriend got so mad at me once because I almost cracked the screen, but I just couldn't look at it. I had to make her pick it up and close the app for me. \n\nAnd the thing is, it doesn't even have to be a real, live animal right in front of me to set me off. Like I was saying before with the moth at the movies... I know a bat in the store probably isn't going to hurt me. Logically, I know that. I'm a grown man, it's a tiny animal, it's probably just looking for bugs or trying to get back outside. But if I see anything that looks like one, or even a quick flutter in the back hallway near the loading dock, my mind just treats it like I'm in real danger. \n\nIt's like my brain completely short-circuits. All logic just goes right out the window. I'll be standing there, sorting inventory or breaking down boxes, totally fine, and then boom—a picture on a screen, a shadow, a quick movement out of the corner of my eye—and my heart is pounding in my ears and every instinct I have is just screaming at me to run for my life. It's exhausting, honestly. You'd think I'd be able to tell myself, 'Hey, it's just a video, it's just a piece of cardboard, it's just a shadow,' but nope. The panic just hits me like a freight train before I can even process what I'm actually looking at.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen to you involving a bat in the past that you think might have triggered this fear?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually, there was. It hasn't always been like this, you know? Like, growing up, I never even thought about them. If you'd asked me three years ago, I wouldn't have cared at all. But this whole thing... it started about two years ago. \n\nI was working a late receiving shift at the store. We get these huge freight trucks in on Tuesday nights, and usually, it's just me and maybe one other guy, unloading pallets to get the floor ready for Wednesday morning. We were in the back corridor—it’s this long, really narrow hallway that connects the loading dock to the main stockroom, right? And the lighting back there is just terrible, just these old, dusty fluorescent tubes that hum and flicker constantly. It feels like a basement even though it's on the ground floor. \n\nAnyway, the other guy had propped the exterior dock door open because it was late summer and the AC in the back is basically nonexistent. We were just trying to get some cross-breeze going. So I'm pushing this heavy U-boat cart full of, I don't know, I think it was giant bottles of laundry detergent, just sweating through my uniform shirt, minding my own business. And suddenly I hear this weird, frantic fluttering sound. At first, I thought maybe a bird got in, or a giant moth, but then it swooped down right over my head. \n\nIt was a bat. It must have flown in through the open dock door looking for bugs or something, and it got trapped in that back corridor with me. And because the hallway is so narrow, it had nowhere to go. It was just darting back and forth, bouncing off the cinderblock walls, swooping down... it felt like it was inches from my face. I could literally feel the air moving from its wings. I completely lost it. I dropped the cart, detergent bottles went crashing everywhere, spilling all over the concrete, and I just bolted. I practically knocked over a stack of empty pallets running out of there. \n\nI couldn't go back in for the rest of the night. My manager was pretty ticked off about the mess, but I physically could not force myself to walk back into that corridor. So, yeah, this has been going on for about two years now, ever since that exact night it got into the corridor during that receiving shift. It's like a switch flipped in my brain right then and there, and I just haven't been the same since.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have this intense fear reaction if you were just thinking about bats, without seeing one or any visual cue?\n\nPatient: Just thinking about them? Yeah. Yeah, absolutely. Honestly, that’s... that’s actually a huge part of what brings me in to see you. Because it’s not just when I see one or see a picture of one. The second I even just think there might be a bat, or if I just imagine something flapping around the stockroom, my body completely reacts. \n\nLike, I don't even need a visual cue at all. I'll just be standing there in the back, maybe doing inventory, counting boxes of paper towels or whatever, and the thought will just randomly pop into my head—'what if there's one up in the rafters right now?' And instantly, my heart jumps. It just starts pounding out of nowhere. I start sweating, like, really sweating through my uniform shirt, and I get this intense, overwhelming feeling that I need to get out right then. I just feel this massive urge to drop whatever I'm holding and escape through the nearest door. \n\nAnd there’s nothing even there! I'm just looking at my clipboard, totally alone in the aisle, but because my brain decided to think about it, I'm practically having a meltdown. My manager, Greg, he’s even caught me a couple of times just standing near the loading dock exit, trying to catch my breath, because I got so worked up over literally nothing but my own thoughts. I have to step outside and just pace the alleyway for ten minutes. It’s like my imagination is just as bad as the real thing, you know? I just picture it, and my brain sends the exact same panic signals, and it just hijacks my whole system before I can even tell myself to calm down. So, yeah, just the thought of them is enough to completely ruin my shift.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this intense fear when thinking about or seeing other flying creatures, like insects?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, bugs are just bugs, you know? Like, working back in receiving, especially when we have those big exterior dock doors propped open in the middle of summer, we get all kinds of stuff flying in. We get flies, mosquitoes, sometimes those massive June bugs that just smack into the fluorescent light tubes and make that loud clicking noise. \n\nAnd wasps! Oh man, last summer we actually had this huge wasp nest form right above the cardboard compactor out in the back alley. Greg, my manager, he actually made me go out there with, like, three cans of that foaming Raid stuff to take care of it because nobody else wanted to do it. I was out there spraying them, and they were swarming everywhere, flying right by my ears, and I didn't care at all. I mean, sure, nobody exactly *likes* the idea of getting stung, but I wasn't having a panic attack about it, you know? My heart wasn't pounding in my chest, I didn't feel like I was in mortal danger, and I definitely didn't drop everything and run away. I just handled it. \n\nEven with that moth at the movie theater I mentioned earlier... the only reason I completely freaked out was because, for that one split second, the way it fluttered in the dark, my brain instantly went 'Bat!' But the absolute second the projector light hit it and I could clearly see it was just a moth, the panic just completely evaporated. I was just like, 'Oh, okay, it's just a bug,' and I was totally fine to sit there and finish the movie. \n\nSame thing with birds. We get pigeons in the store sometimes, they just wander in through the front automatic doors and start walking around the seasonal aisles looking for dropped popcorn or whatever. I'll literally just grab a push broom and casually shoo them back outside. No big deal at all. So, yeah, it's definitely not just things that fly. If it's a bird or an insect, I'm totally normal, completely fine. It's literally just bats. If it's not a bat, I don't bat an eye—no pun intended, I guess. It's just that one specific animal that makes my brain completely short-circuit.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a similar intense fear reaction in a situation that didn't involve bats, but perhaps involved being confined or surprised by something else?\n\nPatient: No, no, I really haven't. It's funny you ask that, actually, because people always assume it's about the small space, right? Like, they think I have claustrophobia or something because of that narrow back corridor where the whole thing started. But it's really not about being trapped or surprised in general. \n\nLike, take our stockroom, for example. We have these heavy-duty rolling track shelves for the overstock—mostly seasonal stuff, you know, plastic pumpkins, patio cushions, that kind of thing. You have to physically turn this big metal crank on the end to open up an aisle to walk down. And sometimes, the younger guys on the shift, they think it's hilarious to crank the aisle shut when you're in there looking for something. So you're suddenly sandwiched between these massive walls of cardboard boxes, and it is incredibly cramped. I mean, you can barely turn your shoulders. You're literally trapped in there until they decide to open it back up. And when they do that to me, I don't panic at all. I just bang on the shelf and yell at them to knock it off. My heart doesn't race, I don't start sweating... I just get annoyed. \n\nAnd as for being surprised by things? Man, working retail, you get surprised all the time. Just last week, actually, I was coming out of the breakroom with my coffee—just a regular black coffee, I always grab one before we start unloading the Tuesday truck—and Greg, my manager, comes practically sprinting around the corner with this huge stack of promotional cardboard signs for the new endcaps. We completely collided. Coffee went everywhere, signs sliding all over the linoleum. It startled the absolute heck out of me, obviously. I jumped out of my skin. But it was just a normal jump, you know? Like a regular 'whoa, watch out!' kind of reaction. \n\nIt wasn't that cold, gripping terror. I didn't feel like my life was in danger. I didn't have that overwhelming urge to just drop everything and run out the fire exit. I just grabbed a roll of paper towels from the breakroom and helped him clean up the mess. So, yeah, if I get stuck somewhere tight, or if someone jumps out at me, or if a stack of heavy boxes suddenly falls off a top shelf right next to my head... I react exactly like any normal person would. A little jump, maybe a curse word, and then I move on. That total system override, that absolute meltdown where my brain just completely hijacks my body and screams at me to run for my life? No, that is one hundred percent exclusive to the bats. Nothing else in the world does that to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any fear or anxiety related to other animals, besides bats, that you think might be connected to this intense fear you have?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, no. It's really just the bats. I've never had a problem with dogs or cats or, I don't know, snakes or anything like that. I actually really like dogs. My sister has this golden retriever mix, Buster, and he's just the sweetest thing. Sometimes he gets a little too excited and jumps up on you when you walk in the door, but it doesn't scare me at all, you know? I just kind of push him off and give him a pat. \n\nAnd even out at the store, sometimes people bring their pets in. Technically, they're only supposed to bring service animals, but you know how it is, people bring in these little yappy dogs in their purses or whatever. And sometimes they bark right as you're walking past them in the aisle, just out of nowhere, and yeah, it's startling, but it's not that same kind of fear. It's not that deep, paralyzing panic where I feel like I have to run away. It's just a regular, 'oh, hey, a dog barked' kind of thing. \n\nI've even seen mice in the stockroom before. We had a bit of a problem with them last winter, actually, getting into the birdseed bags we keep over in the garden section. Greg had to set out all these traps, and I'd be the one who had to go check them in the mornings. And I mean, nobody really likes finding a dead mouse, or seeing one scurry across the floor, but it didn't freak me out. I didn't start sweating or having my heart pound. I just grabbed a broom and swept it up, or told Greg where the live ones were so he could deal with them. \n\nSo, yeah, it's really, truly just the bats. There's no other animal that makes me feel even remotely close to how I feel when I think about or see a bat. It's like they exist in this completely separate category in my brain, and everything else is just... normal. I don't know why it's just them, but it is. I can handle anything else on four legs, or no legs, or whatever. Just not the ones with wings that flutter around in the dark.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Greg had to set out all these traps, and I'd be the one who had to go check them in the mornings. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It's definitely the movement, mostly. That... that fluttering. But it's not like a bird fluttering, you know? It's this jerky, chaotic, unpredictable swooping thing they do. And their shape, too, just that dark, leathery look. 

I mean, logically, sitting here right now talking to you, I know a bat in the store probably isn't going to hurt me. Like, I'm a thirty-seven-year-old man, it's a tiny little animal that eats mosquitoes, right? What's it really going to do to me? I know that in my rational brain. I know the statistics, I know they're more scared of us than we are of them, all that stuff people always tell you. 

But if I see anything that even remotely looks like one, or if there's even just a quick flutter in the back hallway by the loading dock where the fluorescent lighting is always kind of flickering and terrible... my mind just completely treats it like I'm in real danger. Like, actual, immediate, life-or-death danger. 

It's like a switch flips and all that logic about it just being a harmless little critter goes straight out the window. My brain doesn't say, oh hey, there's a bat, let's go tell Greg and call animal control. My brain just screams at me that I am in imminent peril and I need to survive. It's so frustrating because I can tell myself all day long that it's fine, but the absolute second I see that specific shape or that weird, frantic wing movement in the shadows, my body just reacts like I'm staring down a lion or something. It's just this total, overwhelming sense of danger that I completely cannot talk myself out of, no matter how hard I try to be rational about it.

It's so frustrating because I can tell myself all day long that it's fine, but the absolute second I see that specific shape or that weird, frantic wing movement in the shadows, my body just reacts like I'm staring down a lion or something. It's just this total, overwhelming sense of danger that I completely cannot talk myself out of, no matter how hard I try to be rational about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Greg had to set out all these traps, and I'd be the one who had to go check them in the mornings. And sometimes, the younger guys on the shift, they think it's hilarious to crank the aisle shut when you're in there looking for something. So you're suddenly sandwiched between these massive walls of cardboard boxes, and it is incredibly cramped. I mean, you can barely turn your shoulders. You're literally trapped in there until they decide to open it back up. And when they do that to me, I don't panic at all. I just bang on the shelf and yell at them to knock it off. My heart doesn't race, I don't start sweating... I just get annoyed. \n\nAnd as for being surprised by things? Man, working retail, you get surprised all the time. Just last week, actually, I was coming out of the breakroom with my coffee—just a regular black coffee, I always grab one before we start unloading the Tuesday truck—and Greg, my manager, comes practically sprinting around the corner with this huge stack of promotional cardboard signs for the new endcaps. We completely collided. Coffee went everywhere, signs sliding all over the linoleum. It startled the absolute heck out of me, obviously. I jumped out of my skin. But it was just a normal jump, you know? Like a regular 'whoa, watch out!' kind of reaction. \n\nIt wasn't that cold, gripping terror. I didn't feel like my life was in danger. I didn't have that overwhelming urge to just drop everything and run out the fire exit. I just grabbed a roll of paper towels from the breakroom and helped him clean up the mess. So, yeah, if I get stuck somewhere tight, or if someone jumps out at me, or if a stack of heavy boxes suddenly falls off a top shelf right next to my head... I react exactly like any normal person would. A little jump, maybe a curse word, and then I move on. That total system override, that absolute meltdown where my brain just completely hijacks my body and screams at me to run for my life? No, that is one hundred percent exclusive to the bats. Nothing else in the world does that to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any fear or anxiety related to other animals, besides bats, that you think might be connected to this intense fear you have?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, no. It's really just the bats. I've never had a problem with dogs or cats or, I don't know, snakes or anything like that. I actually really like dogs. My sister has this golden retriever mix, Buster, and he's just the sweetest thing. Sometimes he gets a little too excited and jumps up on you when you walk in the door, but it doesn't scare me at all, you know? I just kind of push him off and give him a pat. \n\nAnd even out at the store, sometimes people bring their pets in. Technically, they're only supposed to bring service animals, but you know how it is, people bring in these little yappy dogs in their purses or whatever. And sometimes they bark right as you're walking past them in the aisle, just out of nowhere, and yeah, it's startling, but it's not that same kind of fear. It's not that deep, paralyzing panic where I feel like I have to run away. It's just a regular, 'oh, hey, a dog barked' kind of thing. \n\nI've even seen mice in the stockroom before. We had a bit of a problem with them last winter, actually, getting into the birdseed bags we keep over in the garden section. Greg had to set out all these traps, and I'd be the one who had to go check them in the mornings. And I mean, nobody really likes finding a dead mouse, or seeing one scurry across the floor, but it didn't freak me out. I didn't start sweating or having my heart pound. I just grabbed a broom and swept it up, or told Greg where the live ones were so he could deal with them. \n\nSo, yeah, it's really, truly just the bats. There's no other animal that makes me feel even remotely close to how I feel when I think about or see a bat. It's like they exist in this completely separate category in my brain, and everything else is just... normal. I don't know why it's just them, but it is. I can handle anything else on four legs, or no legs, or whatever. Just not the ones with wings that flutter around in the dark.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything specific about bats, such as their appearance, sound, or movement, that triggers your fear reaction?\n\nPatient: It's definitely the movement, mostly. That... that fluttering. But it's not like a bird fluttering, you know? It's this jerky, chaotic, unpredictable swooping thing they do. And their shape, too, just that dark, leathery look. \n\nI mean, logically, sitting here right now talking to you, I know a bat in the store probably isn't going to hurt me. Like, I'm a thirty-seven-year-old man, it's a tiny little animal that eats mosquitoes, right? What's it really going to do to me? I know that in my rational brain. I know the statistics, I know they're more scared of us than we are of them, all that stuff people always tell you. \n\nBut if I see anything that even remotely looks like one, or if there's even just a quick flutter in the back hallway by the loading dock where the fluorescent lighting is always kind of flickering and terrible... my mind just completely treats it like I'm in real danger. Like, actual, immediate, life-or-death danger. \n\nIt's like a switch flips and all that logic about it just being a harmless little critter goes straight out the window. My brain doesn't say, oh hey, there's a bat, let's go tell Greg and call animal control. My brain just screams at me that I am in imminent peril and I need to survive. It's so frustrating because I can tell myself all day long that it's fine, but the absolute second I see that specific shape or that weird, frantic wing movement in the shadows, my body just reacts like I'm staring down a lion or something. It's just this total, overwhelming sense of danger that I completely cannot talk myself out of, no matter how hard I try to be rational about it.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs there anything about other flying animals, such as their movement or appearance, that triggers a similar fear reaction in you?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no, not at all. Birds don't bother me one bit. It's funny you ask, actually, because my neighbor—Mrs. Higgins, she lives two houses down from me—she has this massive bird feeder setup in her backyard. I mean, it's like a whole avian buffet out there. She's got the suet cakes, the black oil sunflower seeds, the thistle feeders for the finches... the whole nine yards. And my bedroom window looks right out over her yard. So, especially in the mornings, I see birds swooping around all the time. Cardinals, blue jays, chickadees, you name it. They're constantly darting in and out, fluttering around, sometimes they even chase each other right past my window. 

And it doesn't phase me in the slightest. I actually kind of like watching them while I drink my coffee before I head into the store. Their movement is just... different. It's graceful, you know? Even when they're flying fast or changing direction quickly, it makes sense to my brain. It's a bird doing bird things. It's not that erratic, jerky, unpredictable flapping that bats do. 

And as for bugs? Like, flying insects? Man, working in the garden center sometimes during the summer, you get all sorts of things flying at your face. We get these huge moths sometimes, especially around the outdoor floodlights when we're closing up at night. And yeah, they flutter, and they can be kind of gross when they dive-bomb your forehead, but it's just annoying. I just swat them away. Same with bees or wasps. If a wasp flies near me, I might take a step back because I don't want to get stung, but it's a totally rational reaction. It's not that cold, absolute panic. 

I've even had a bird get stuck in the store before! Last spring, a sparrow somehow got through the automatic doors up front and ended up flying all the way back to the hardware aisles. It was fluttering around up near the ceiling, clearly panicked, trying to find a way out. And everyone else was kind of ducking and pointing at it, but I wasn't scared at all. I actually felt bad for the poor thing. Greg and I spent like twenty minutes trying to herd it back toward the front doors with a couple of pool noodles we grabbed from seasonal. It was swooping all over the place, sometimes coming pretty close to our heads, but my heart rate didn't even go up. I was just focused on helping it get outside. 

So, yeah, it's really not just the flying aspect, or the fluttering, or things moving fast in the air. If it's a bird, a moth, a butterfly, whatever... totally fine. It is exclusively, one hundred percent, just the bats. There's just something so specific about the way they look and the way they move that my brain just absolutely cannot handle. Everything else with wings gets a total pass.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F40.231\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it's... it's kind of embarrassing, honestly, but my manager actually suggested I come see someone. I work retail down at the big hardware store on 4th Street, you know the one? Huge place, high ceilings, lots of dark corners in the back. I've been there for about five years now, mostly on the floor, but sometimes they need me to pull inventory from the stockroom. And that's... that's where the problem is. See, the stockroom is massive, and sometimes things get in there. Birds, mostly, but... well, what brings me in is that the second I even *think* there might be a bat or something flapping around back there, it's like a switch flips in my body. My heart just jumps right into my throat, I mean it starts pounding so hard I can hear it in my ears, and I start sweating through my uniform shirt. It's not just a little nervous, it's this overwhelming, immediate feeling that I have to get out of that room right then and there. I can't even think straight, I just have to drop whatever I'm doing and leave, which, as you can imagine, is really starting to mess with my job. I mean, just last week, a moth flew past the forklift and I nearly knocked over a whole pallet of paint cans trying to get to the door...\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had one of these episodes where you felt like you were going to pass out or thought you were having a heart attack?\n\nPatient: Pass out? No, I... no, I don't think so. And I don't think I'm having a heart attack, either. I mean, my heart is going a mile a minute, like I was saying, just hammering away against my ribs, but I never actually think, 'Oh, this is it, I'm having a heart attack.' It's not like that heavy chest pain you see on TV or anything. My uncle actually had a heart attack a few years back while he was out fixing his truck, and he said it felt like a cinderblock was sitting right on his chest. \n\nFor me, it's not a medical emergency kind of feeling, it's more like... well, it's just this intense, overwhelming urge to run. Like my legs just absolutely have to move right that second. I don't get lightheaded or feel like my vision is going dark or that I'm going to faint, either. If anything, I feel way too awake, you know? Like every single nerve in my body is suddenly electrified and screaming at me to get out of that stockroom before something swoops down and gets tangled in my hair or something. So, no, definitely no passing out. I just feel like I need to sprint in the opposite direction as fast as humanly possible, even if it means dropping whatever I'm holding right there on the concrete floor and leaving a mess for the shift supervisor to find.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the thought of bats or other animals in the stockroom that triggers this reaction, or would any potentially frightening situation cause the same response?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely just the bats, or, you know, things that I think are bats. It's not like I'm just a generally jumpy guy or afraid of everything. I mean, a few months ago, a pallet of soil bags collapsed right near me in the garden center—loud crash, dirt everywhere, total chaos—and I didn't panic at all. I just grabbed a broom and started cleaning it up. But the bats... man, it's so specific. \n\nAnd the thing is, logically, I know a bat in the store probably isn't going to hurt me. I mean, what's a little brown bat going to do to a guy my size, right? They eat bugs, they don't want anything to do with people. My buddy Dave, he actually loves them, says they're great for mosquito control around his porch. I get all that in my head. I really do. But the second I see anything that looks like one, or even just a quick flutter of movement in the back hallway by the loading dock, my mind just throws logic right out the window. \n\nIt treats it like I'm in actual, immediate danger. Like a life-or-death situation. It doesn't matter if it turns out to just be a piece of black plastic caught in the draft from the AC vents—which happened last Tuesday, by the way. I saw this dark shape kind of bobbing near the ceiling, and my brain just screamed 'bat!' and before I even knew what I was doing, I had backed into a display of caulking guns. It's just this instant, overpowering reaction to that one specific thing, no matter how much I try to tell myself it's harmless. It's so frustrating because I know it doesn't make sense, but my body just reacts like there's a tiger in the room with me.\n\nDoctor: Does this intense fear of bats happen to you only when you're in the stockroom or can it occur in other places as well?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's definitely not just the stockroom. I mean, that's where it's been causing the most trouble lately because of work, but no, it happens anywhere. Anywhere at all. Like, last summer, my sister had this barbecue at her place out in the suburbs—nice house, big backyard, string lights, the whole nine yards. We were all sitting around the fire pit, roasting marshmallows, having a couple of beers, just a really nice evening. The sun was going down, you know, that kind of dusky purple sky? And suddenly, this... this thing swoops over the yard. \n\nMy brother-in-law, Mark, he just casually goes, 'Oh, look, a bat, catching mosquitoes.' Doctor, I didn't even wait to see it again. I dropped my skewer—marshmallow went right into the dirt—and I practically sprinted inside the house. I slammed the sliding glass door so hard I thought it was going to shatter. I locked it, too, which made my sister pretty mad because everyone else was still out there. I ended up spending the rest of the night sitting in her kitchen, watching them through the window, sweating and shaking, just waiting for it to come back. \n\nAnd it's not just outside, either. I was at the movies a couple of years ago, seeing one of those superhero flicks with my buddy Dave. We were in the big theater, stadium seating, and right in the middle of a quiet scene, a moth—a really big moth, I guess—got caught in the projector beam. Just this huge, fluttering shadow on the screen. My brain instantly went, 'Bat!' and I grabbed my jacket and bolted down the aisle. I didn't even tell Dave I was leaving. I just sat in my car in the parking lot for an hour until the movie was over. So, yeah, it doesn't matter where I am. If I see one, or even think I see one, I just have to get away, no matter who I'm with or what I'm doing. It's embarrassing, really, having to explain to people why a grown man just ran away from a shadow, but I just can't control it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar intense fear reaction to any other animals, such as spiders, snakes, or rodents?\n\nPatient: Spiders? Snakes? No, no, nothing like that. I mean, sure, I'm not exactly thrilled if I find a spider in the bathtub, you know? Like, last week I was getting ready for work, just turning on the shower, and there was this big, hairy wolf spider right near the drain. I didn't love it, but I just grabbed a cup from the sink, scooped it up, and tossed it out the window into the bushes. Didn't even really think twice about it. My heart didn't race, I didn't break out into a sweat, I didn't feel like I had to run out of the bathroom and leave the house entirely. It was just a spider. \n\nAnd snakes, well, I actually think they're kind of neat. When I was a kid, maybe ten or eleven, my dad took me hiking up near the state park, and we saw this garter snake sunning itself on a rock. I actually wanted to get closer to look at its patterns. I'd never want one as a pet or anything—feeding them mice seems like a hassle—but they don't bother me. Same with rodents. We had a mouse in the breakroom at work a couple of years ago. It was getting into the vending machine snacks. Some of the cashiers were freaking out, standing on chairs, the whole bit, but I just set a few traps and checked them every morning. When I finally caught the little guy, it didn't bother me at all to dispose of it. \n\nSo, yeah, it really is just... just the bats. It's so weird, right? Like, why bats? Why not something that actually bites people a lot, like stray dogs or something? But no, a stray dog wanders into the parking lot, I'm the first one out there trying to coax it over with a piece of beef jerky so I can check its tags. But if a bat even flies near the building, I'm a complete wreck. It's just that one specific thing, nothing else even comes close to making me feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar intense fear reaction to other flying creatures, like birds?\n\nPatient: Birds? No, no, definitely not. It's funny you bring that up, actually, because people always assume that. They think, 'Oh, he hates bats, he must hate anything with wings,' right? But birds are completely fine. I don't mind them at all. \n\nActually, just last spring, we had this little bird—I think it was a sparrow, or maybe a finch, just a little brown guy—get trapped inside the store. It must have flown in through the main automatic doors when someone was pushing out a cart of patio furniture. Anyway, this poor thing is flying all around the high ceilings over by Aisle 4, right above the plumbing fixtures and PVC pipes. It was fluttering around, bumping into the fluorescent lights, clearly terrified. Now, you’d think a small, dark thing flying around indoors would set me off, but I was totally calm. I actually felt really bad for it. I was the one who went all the way over to the seasonal section, grabbed one of those long pool skimmer nets, and spent, I don't know, maybe forty-five minutes trying to gently guide it back toward the front of the store. I was literally standing on a step ladder, talking to it, saying, 'Come on buddy, the exit is right over there.' My heart wasn't racing, I wasn't sweating... nothing. I even got close enough to see its little feathers when it stopped to rest on a sign. \n\nSo, yeah, birds don't bother me one bit. Same with pigeons in the parking lot, or seagulls. I went to Ocean City a few years back and those gulls are huge, they'll swoop right down and steal a french fry right out of your hand if you're not paying attention. Annoying? Sure. But scary? Not at all. It's just... it doesn't trigger that alarm bell in my head. If that sparrow in Aisle 4 had been a bat, though? I would have left my shift right then and there. I would have been halfway down the highway before my manager even knew I was gone. So it really is just the bats, nothing else with wings does that to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had this intense fear reaction when seeing a picture or a video of a bat, rather than a real one?\n\nPatient: Pictures or videos? Oh, man, yeah. It's... it's actually ridiculous how bad it is. Like, around Halloween, right? Working in retail, you know how it is, we start putting out the seasonal stuff in, like, late August. And there are always these cardboard cutouts, or those motion-activated props, or even just the graphics on the big bags of candy. My manager, Greg, he knows I hate them, but corporate sends what corporate sends, so we have to put them on the endcaps. And even just seeing a picture of one on the packaging... I have to look away. I get this awful tightness in my chest and my hands get all clammy. \n\nOr if I'm just sitting at home, scrolling on my phone, you know, watching some random video on social media or whatever, and suddenly a clip from a nature documentary pops up on the screen? I've literally thrown my phone across the couch. My girlfriend got so mad at me once because I almost cracked the screen, but I just couldn't look at it. I had to make her pick it up and close the app for me. \n\nAnd the thing is, it doesn't even have to be a real, live animal right in front of me to set me off. Like I was saying before with the moth at the movies... I know a bat in the store probably isn't going to hurt me. Logically, I know that. I'm a grown man, it's a tiny animal, it's probably just looking for bugs or trying to get back outside. But if I see anything that looks like one, or even a quick flutter in the back hallway near the loading dock, my mind just treats it like I'm in real danger. \n\nIt's like my brain completely short-circuits. All logic just goes right out the window. I'll be standing there, sorting inventory or breaking down boxes, totally fine, and then boom—a picture on a screen, a shadow, a quick movement out of the corner of my eye—and my heart is pounding in my ears and every instinct I have is just screaming at me to run for my life. It's exhausting, honestly. You'd think I'd be able to tell myself, 'Hey, it's just a video, it's just a piece of cardboard, it's just a shadow,' but nope. The panic just hits me like a freight train before I can even process what I'm actually looking at.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen to you involving a bat in the past that you think might have triggered this fear?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually, there was. It hasn't always been like this, you know? Like, growing up, I never even thought about them. If you'd asked me three years ago, I wouldn't have cared at all. But this whole thing... it started about two years ago. \n\nI was working a late receiving shift at the store. We get these huge freight trucks in on Tuesday nights, and usually, it's just me and maybe one other guy, unloading pallets to get the floor ready for Wednesday morning. We were in the back corridor—it’s this long, really narrow hallway that connects the loading dock to the main stockroom, right? And the lighting back there is just terrible, just these old, dusty fluorescent tubes that hum and flicker constantly. It feels like a basement even though it's on the ground floor. \n\nAnyway, the other guy had propped the exterior dock door open because it was late summer and the AC in the back is basically nonexistent. We were just trying to get some cross-breeze going. So I'm pushing this heavy U-boat cart full of, I don't know, I think it was giant bottles of laundry detergent, just sweating through my uniform shirt, minding my own business. And suddenly I hear this weird, frantic fluttering sound. At first, I thought maybe a bird got in, or a giant moth, but then it swooped down right over my head. \n\nIt was a bat. It must have flown in through the open dock door looking for bugs or something, and it got trapped in that back corridor with me. And because the hallway is so narrow, it had nowhere to go. It was just darting back and forth, bouncing off the cinderblock walls, swooping down... it felt like it was inches from my face. I could literally feel the air moving from its wings. I completely lost it. I dropped the cart, detergent bottles went crashing everywhere, spilling all over the concrete, and I just bolted. I practically knocked over a stack of empty pallets running out of there. \n\nI couldn't go back in for the rest of the night. My manager was pretty ticked off about the mess, but I physically could not force myself to walk back into that corridor. So, yeah, this has been going on for about two years now, ever since that exact night it got into the corridor during that receiving shift. It's like a switch flipped in my brain right then and there, and I just haven't been the same since.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have this intense fear reaction if you were just thinking about bats, without seeing one or any visual cue?\n\nPatient: Just thinking about them? Yeah. Yeah, absolutely. Honestly, that’s... that’s actually a huge part of what brings me in to see you. Because it’s not just when I see one or see a picture of one. The second I even just think there might be a bat, or if I just imagine something flapping around the stockroom, my body completely reacts. \n\nLike, I don't even need a visual cue at all. I'll just be standing there in the back, maybe doing inventory, counting boxes of paper towels or whatever, and the thought will just randomly pop into my head—'what if there's one up in the rafters right now?' And instantly, my heart jumps. It just starts pounding out of nowhere. I start sweating, like, really sweating through my uniform shirt, and I get this intense, overwhelming feeling that I need to get out right then. I just feel this massive urge to drop whatever I'm holding and escape through the nearest door. \n\nAnd there’s nothing even there! I'm just looking at my clipboard, totally alone in the aisle, but because my brain decided to think about it, I'm practically having a meltdown. My manager, Greg, he’s even caught me a couple of times just standing near the loading dock exit, trying to catch my breath, because I got so worked up over literally nothing but my own thoughts. I have to step outside and just pace the alleyway for ten minutes. It’s like my imagination is just as bad as the real thing, you know? I just picture it, and my brain sends the exact same panic signals, and it just hijacks my whole system before I can even tell myself to calm down. So, yeah, just the thought of them is enough to completely ruin my shift.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this intense fear when thinking about or seeing other flying creatures, like insects?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, bugs are just bugs, you know? Like, working back in receiving, especially when we have those big exterior dock doors propped open in the middle of summer, we get all kinds of stuff flying in. We get flies, mosquitoes, sometimes those massive June bugs that just smack into the fluorescent light tubes and make that loud clicking noise. \n\nAnd wasps! Oh man, last summer we actually had this huge wasp nest form right above the cardboard compactor out in the back alley. I was out there spraying them, and they were swarming everywhere, flying right by my ears, and I didn't care at all. I mean, sure, nobody exactly *likes* the idea of getting stung, but I wasn't having a panic attack about it, you know? My heart wasn't pounding in my chest, I didn't feel like I was in mortal danger, and I definitely didn't drop everything and run away. I just handled it. \n\nEven with that moth at the movie theater I mentioned earlier... the only reason I completely freaked out was because, for that one split second, the way it fluttered in the dark, my brain instantly went 'Bat!' But the absolute second the projector light hit it and I could clearly see it was just a moth, the panic just completely evaporated. I was just like, 'Oh, okay, it's just a bug,' and I was totally fine to sit there and finish the movie. \n\nSame thing with birds. We get pigeons in the store sometimes, they just wander in through the front automatic doors and start walking around the seasonal aisles looking for dropped popcorn or whatever. I'll literally just grab a push broom and casually shoo them back outside. No big deal at all. So, yeah, it's definitely not just things that fly. If it's a bird or an insect, I'm totally normal, completely fine. It's literally just bats. If it's not a bat, I don't bat an eye—no pun intended, I guess. It's just that one specific animal that makes my brain completely short-circuit.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a similar intense fear reaction in a situation that didn't involve bats, but perhaps involved being confined or surprised by something else?\n\nPatient: No, no, I really haven't. It's funny you ask that, actually, because people always assume it's about the small space, right? Like, they think I have claustrophobia or something because of that narrow back corridor where the whole thing started. But it's really not about being trapped or surprised in general. \n\nLike, take our stockroom, for example. We have these heavy-duty rolling track shelves for the overstock—mostly seasonal stuff, you know, plastic pumpkins, patio cushions, that kind of thing. You have to physically turn this big metal crank on the end to open up an aisle to walk down. And sometimes, the younger guys on the shift, they think it's hilarious to crank the aisle shut when you're in there looking for something. So you're suddenly sandwiched between these massive walls of cardboard boxes, and it is incredibly cramped. I mean, you can barely turn your shoulders. You're literally trapped in there until they decide to open it back up. And when they do that to me, I don't panic at all. I just bang on the shelf and yell at them to knock it off. My heart doesn't race, I don't start sweating... I just get annoyed. \n\nAnd as for being surprised by things? Man, working retail, you get surprised all the time. Just last week, actually, I was coming out of the breakroom with my coffee—just a regular black coffee, I always grab one before we start unloading the Tuesday truck—and Greg, my manager, comes practically sprinting around the corner with this huge stack of promotional cardboard signs for the new endcaps. We completely collided. Coffee went everywhere, signs sliding all over the linoleum. It startled the absolute heck out of me, obviously. I jumped out of my skin. But it was just a normal jump, you know? Like a regular 'whoa, watch out!' kind of reaction. \n\nIt wasn't that cold, gripping terror. I didn't feel like my life was in danger. I didn't have that overwhelming urge to just drop everything and run out the fire exit. I just grabbed a roll of paper towels from the breakroom and helped him clean up the mess. I react exactly like any normal person would. A little jump, maybe a curse word, and then I move on. That total system override, that absolute meltdown where my brain just completely hijacks my body and screams at me to run for my life? No, that is one hundred percent exclusive to the bats. Nothing else in the world does that to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any fear or anxiety related to other animals, besides bats, that you think might be connected to this intense fear you have?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, no. It's really just the bats. I've never had a problem with dogs or cats or, I don't know, snakes or anything like that. I actually really like dogs. My sister has this golden retriever mix, Buster, and he's just the sweetest thing. Sometimes he gets a little too excited and jumps up on you when you walk in the door, but it doesn't scare me at all, you know? I just kind of push him off and give him a pat. \n\nAnd even out at the store, sometimes people bring their pets in. And sometimes they bark right as you're walking past them in the aisle, just out of nowhere, and yeah, it's startling, but it's not that same kind of fear. It's not that deep, paralyzing panic where I feel like I have to run away. It's just a regular, 'oh, hey, a dog barked' kind of thing. \n\nI've even seen mice in the stockroom before. We had a bit of a problem with them last winter, actually, getting into the birdseed bags we keep over in the garden section. Greg had to set out all these traps, and I'd be the one who had to go check them in the mornings. And I mean, nobody really likes finding a dead mouse, or seeing one scurry across the floor, but it didn't freak me out. I didn't start sweating or having my heart pound. I just grabbed a broom and swept it up, or told Greg where the live ones were so he could deal with them. \n\nSo, yeah, it's really, truly just the bats. There's no other animal that makes me feel even remotely close to how I feel when I think about or see a bat. It's like they exist in this completely separate category in my brain, and everything else is just... normal. I don't know why it's just them, but it is. I can handle anything else on four legs, or no legs, or whatever. Like, actual, immediate, life-or-death danger. \n\nIt's like a switch flips and all that logic about it just being a harmless little critter goes straight out the window. My brain doesn't say, oh hey, there's a bat, let's go tell Greg and call animal control. My brain just screams at me that I am in imminent peril and I need to survive. It's so frustrating because I can tell myself all day long that it's fine, but the absolute second I see that specific shape or that weird, frantic wing movement in the shadows, my body just reacts like I'm staring down a lion or something. I mean, it's like a whole avian buffet out there. She's got the suet cakes, the black oil sunflower seeds, the thistle feeders for the finches... the whole nine yards. And my bedroom window looks right out over her yard. So, especially in the mornings, I see birds swooping around all the time. Cardinals, blue jays, chickadees, you name it. They're constantly darting in and out, fluttering around, sometimes they even chase each other right past my window. \n\nAnd it doesn't phase me in the slightest. I was just focused on helping it get outside. \n\nSo, yeah, it's really not just the flying aspect, or the fluttering, or things moving fast in the air. It hasn't always been like this, you know? Like, growing up, I never even thought about them. If you'd asked me three years ago, I wouldn't have cared at all. But this whole thing... it started about two years ago. \n\nI was working a late receiving shift at the store. We get these huge freight trucks in on Tuesday nights, and usually, it's just me and maybe one other guy, unloading pallets to get the floor ready for Wednesday morning. We were in the back corridor—it’s this long, really narrow hallway that connects the loading dock to the main stockroom, right? And the lighting back there is just terrible, just these old, dusty fluorescent tubes that hum and flicker constantly. It feels like a basement even though it's on the ground floor. \n\nAnyway, the other guy had propped the exterior dock door open because it was late summer and the AC in the back is basically nonexistent. We were just trying to get some cross-breeze going. So I'm pushing this heavy U-boat cart full of, I don't know, I think it was giant bottles of laundry detergent, just sweating through my uniform shirt, minding my own business. And suddenly I hear this weird, frantic fluttering sound. At first, I thought maybe a bird got in, or a giant moth, but then it swooped down right over my head. \n\nIt was a bat. It must have flown in through the open dock door looking for bugs or something, and it got trapped in that back corridor with me. And because the hallway is so narrow, it had nowhere to go. It was just darting back and forth, bouncing off the cinderblock walls, swooping down... it felt like it was inches from my face. I could literally feel the air moving from its wings. I completely lost it. I dropped the cart, detergent bottles went crashing everywhere, spilling all over the concrete, and I just bolted. I practically knocked over a stack of empty pallets running out of there. \n\nI couldn't go back in for the rest of the night. My manager was pretty ticked off about the mess, but I physically could not force myself to walk back into that corridor. So, yeah, this has been going on for about two years now, ever since that exact night it got into the corridor during that receiving shift. It's like a switch flipped in my brain right then and there, and I just haven't been the same since.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have this intense fear reaction if you were just thinking about bats, without seeing one or any visual cue?\n\nPatient: Just thinking about them? Yeah. Yeah, absolutely. Honestly, that’s... that’s actually a huge part of what brings me in to see you. Because it’s not just when I see one or see a picture of one. The second I even just think there might be a bat, or if I just imagine something flapping around the stockroom, my body completely reacts. \n\nLike, I don't even need a visual cue at all. I'll just be standing there in the back, maybe doing inventory, counting boxes of paper towels or whatever, and the thought will just randomly pop into my head—'what if there's one up in the rafters right now?' And instantly, my heart jumps. It just starts pounding out of nowhere. I start sweating, like, really sweating through my uniform shirt, and I get this intense, overwhelming feeling that I need to get out right then. I just feel this massive urge to drop whatever I'm holding and escape through the nearest door. \n\nAnd there’s nothing even there! I'm just looking at my clipboard, totally alone in the aisle, but because my brain decided to think about it, I'm practically having a meltdown. My manager, Greg, he’s even caught me a couple of times just standing near the loading dock exit, trying to catch my breath, because I got so worked up over literally nothing but my own thoughts. I have to step outside and just pace the alleyway for ten minutes. It’s like my imagination is just as bad as the real thing, you know? I just picture it, and my brain sends the exact same panic signals, and it just hijacks my whole system before I can even tell myself to calm down. So, yeah, just the thought of them is enough to completely ruin my shift.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this intense fear when thinking about or seeing other flying creatures, like insects?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, bugs are just bugs, you know? Like, working back in receiving, especially when we have those big exterior dock doors propped open in the middle of summer, we get all kinds of stuff flying in. We get flies, mosquitoes, sometimes those massive June bugs that just smack into the fluorescent light tubes and make that loud clicking noise. \n\nAnd wasps! Oh man, last summer we actually had this huge wasp nest form right above the cardboard compactor out in the back alley. Greg, my manager, he actually made me go out there with, like, three cans of that foaming Raid stuff to take care of it because nobody else wanted to do it. I was out there spraying them, and they were swarming everywhere, flying right by my ears, and I didn't care at all. I mean, sure, nobody exactly *likes* the idea of getting stung, but I wasn't having a panic attack about it, you know? My heart wasn't pounding in my chest, I didn't feel like I was in mortal danger, and I definitely didn't drop everything and run away. I just handled it. \n\nEven with that moth at the movie theater I mentioned earlier... the only reason I completely freaked out was because, for that one split second, the way it fluttered in the dark, my brain instantly went 'Bat!' But the absolute second the projector light hit it and I could clearly see it was just a moth, the panic just completely evaporated. I was just like, 'Oh, okay, it's just a bug,' and I was totally fine to sit there and finish the movie. \n\nSame thing with birds. We get pigeons in the store sometimes, they just wander in through the front automatic doors and start walking around the seasonal aisles looking for dropped popcorn or whatever. I'll literally just grab a push broom and casually shoo them back outside. No big deal at all. So, yeah, it's definitely not just things that fly. If it's a bird or an insect, I'm totally normal, completely fine. It's literally just bats. If it's not a bat, I don't bat an eye—no pun intended, I guess. It's just that one specific animal that makes my brain completely short-circuit.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a similar intense fear reaction in a situation that didn't involve bats, but perhaps involved being confined or surprised by something else?\n\nPatient: No, no, I really haven't. It's funny you ask that, actually, because people always assume it's about the small space, right? Like, they think I have claustrophobia or something because of that narrow back corridor where the whole thing started. But it's really not about being trapped or surprised in general. \n\nLike, take our stockroom, for example. We have these heavy-duty rolling track shelves for the overstock—mostly seasonal stuff, you know, plastic pumpkins, patio cushions, that kind of thing. You have to physically turn this big metal crank on the end to open up an aisle to walk down. And sometimes, the younger guys on the shift, they think it's hilarious to crank the aisle shut when you're in there looking for something. So you're suddenly sandwiched between these massive walls of cardboard boxes, and it is incredibly cramped. I mean, you can barely turn your shoulders. You're literally trapped in there until they decide to open it back up. And when they do that to me, I don't panic at all. I just bang on the shelf and yell at them to knock it off. My heart doesn't race, I don't start sweating... I just get annoyed. \n\nAnd as for being surprised by things? Man, working retail, you get surprised all the time. Just last week, actually, I was coming out of the breakroom with my coffee—just a regular black coffee, I always grab one before we start unloading the Tuesday truck—and Greg, my manager, comes practically sprinting around the corner with this huge stack of promotional cardboard signs for the new endcaps. We completely collided. Coffee went everywhere, signs sliding all over the linoleum. It startled the absolute heck out of me, obviously. I jumped out of my skin. But it was just a normal jump, you know? Like a regular 'whoa, watch out!' kind of reaction. \n\nIt wasn't that cold, gripping terror. I didn't feel like my life was in danger. I didn't have that overwhelming urge to just drop everything and run out the fire exit. I just grabbed a roll of paper towels from the breakroom and helped him clean up the mess. So, yeah, if I get stuck somewhere tight, or if someone jumps out at me, or if a stack of heavy boxes suddenly falls off a top shelf right next to my head... I react exactly like any normal person would. A little jump, maybe a curse word, and then I move on. That total system override, that absolute meltdown where my brain just completely hijacks my body and screams at me to run for my life? No, that is one hundred percent exclusive to the bats. Nothing else in the world does that to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any fear or anxiety related to other animals, besides bats, that you think might be connected to this intense fear you have?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, no. It's really just the bats. I've never had a problem with dogs or cats or, I don't know, snakes or anything like that. I actually really like dogs. My sister has this golden retriever mix, Buster, and he's just the sweetest thing. Sometimes he gets a little too excited and jumps up on you when you walk in the door, but it doesn't scare me at all, you know? I just kind of push him off and give him a pat. \n\nAnd even out at the store, sometimes people bring their pets in. Technically, they're only supposed to bring service animals, but you know how it is, people bring in these little yappy dogs in their purses or whatever. And sometimes they bark right as you're walking past them in the aisle, just out of nowhere, and yeah, it's startling, but it's not that same kind of fear. It's not that deep, paralyzing panic where I feel like I have to run away. It's just a regular, 'oh, hey, a dog barked' kind of thing. \n\nI've even seen mice in the stockroom before. We had a bit of a problem with them last winter, actually, getting into the birdseed bags we keep over in the garden section. Greg had to set out all these traps, and I'd be the one who had to go check them in the mornings. And I mean, nobody really likes finding a dead mouse, or seeing one scurry across the floor, but it didn't freak me out. I didn't start sweating or having my heart pound. I just grabbed a broom and swept it up, or told Greg where the live ones were so he could deal with them. \n\nSo, yeah, it's really, truly just the bats. There's no other animal that makes me feel even remotely close to how I feel when I think about or see a bat. It's like they exist in this completely separate category in my brain, and everything else is just... normal. I don't know why it's just them, but it is. I can handle anything else on four legs, or no legs, or whatever. Just not the ones with wings that flutter around in the dark.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything specific about bats, such as their appearance, sound, or movement, that triggers your fear reaction?\n\nPatient: It's definitely the movement, mostly. That... that fluttering. But it's not like a bird fluttering, you know? It's this jerky, chaotic, unpredictable swooping thing they do. And their shape, too, just that dark, leathery look. \n\nI mean, logically, sitting here right now talking to you, I know a bat in the store probably isn't going to hurt me. Like, I'm a thirty-seven-year-old man, it's a tiny little animal that eats mosquitoes, right? What's it really going to do to me? I know that in my rational brain. I know the statistics, I know they're more scared of us than we are of them, all that stuff people always tell you. \n\nBut if I see anything that even remotely looks like one, or if there's even just a quick flutter in the back hallway by the loading dock where the fluorescent lighting is always kind of flickering and terrible... my mind just completely treats it like I'm in real danger. Like, actual, immediate, life-or-death danger. \n\nIt's like a switch flips and all that logic about it just being a harmless little critter goes straight out the window. My brain doesn't say, oh hey, there's a bat, let's go tell Greg and call animal control. My brain just screams at me that I am in imminent peril and I need to survive. It's so frustrating because I can tell myself all day long that it's fine, but the absolute second I see that specific shape or that weird, frantic wing movement in the shadows, my body just reacts like I'm staring down a lion or something. It's just this total, overwhelming sense of danger that I completely cannot talk myself out of, no matter how hard I try to be rational about it.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything about other flying animals, such as their movement or appearance, that triggers a similar fear reaction in you?\n\nPatient: No, no, not at all. Birds don't bother me one bit. It's funny you ask, actually, because my neighbor—Mrs. Higgins, she lives two houses down from me—she has this massive bird feeder setup in her backyard. I mean, it's like a whole avian buffet out there. She's got the suet cakes, the black oil sunflower seeds, the thistle feeders for the finches... the whole nine yards. And my bedroom window looks right out over her yard. So, especially in the mornings, I see birds swooping around all the time. Cardinals, blue jays, chickadees, you name it. They're constantly darting in and out, fluttering around, sometimes they even chase each other right past my window. \n\nAnd it doesn't phase me in the slightest. I actually kind of like watching them while I drink my coffee before I head into the store. Their movement is just... different. It's graceful, you know? Even when they're flying fast or changing direction quickly, it makes sense to my brain. It's a bird doing bird things. It's not that erratic, jerky, unpredictable flapping that bats do. \n\nAnd as for bugs? Like, flying insects? Man, working in the garden center sometimes during the summer, you get all sorts of things flying at your face. We get these huge moths sometimes, especially around the outdoor floodlights when we're closing up at night. And yeah, they flutter, and they can be kind of gross when they dive-bomb your forehead, but it's just annoying. I just swat them away. Same with bees or wasps. If a wasp flies near me, I might take a step back because I don't want to get stung, but it's a totally rational reaction. It's not that cold, absolute panic. \n\nI've even had a bird get stuck in the store before! Last spring, a sparrow somehow got through the automatic doors up front and ended up flying all the way back to the hardware aisles. It was fluttering around up near the ceiling, clearly panicked, trying to find a way out. And everyone else was kind of ducking and pointing at it, but I wasn't scared at all. I actually felt bad for the poor thing. Greg and I spent like twenty minutes trying to herd it back toward the front doors with a couple of pool noodles we grabbed from seasonal. It was swooping all over the place, sometimes coming pretty close to our heads, but my heart rate didn't even go up. I was just focused on helping it get outside. \n\nSo, yeah, it's really not just the flying aspect, or the fluttering, or things moving fast in the air. If it's a bird, a moth, a butterfly, whatever... totally fine. It is exclusively, one hundred percent, just the bats. There's just something so specific about the way they look and the way they move that my brain just absolutely cannot handle. Everything else with wings gets a total pass.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
You know, thinking back on it... no. Not at all. And I guess that’s what’s so crazy about all of this. Before that, I never really thought about bats one way or another. I mean, maybe I saw them on nature documentaries, or flying way up high against the sky when I was a kid playing outside in the summer, but they never bothered me. They were just... there, you know? Completely off my radar. 

But this whole thing, this intense reaction I have now? It’s really only been going on for about two years. It all traces back exactly to that one specific night. I was working a late receiving shift—which, usually, those are pretty quiet, routine shifts. The delivery trucks usually roll up around eight or nine at night, and we just spend a few hours breaking down pallets in the back corridor before we haul the stock out to the main floor. It's usually just me, maybe one other guy, listening to the radio and cutting open cardboard boxes. 

But that night, that one specific receiving shift two years ago, that bat somehow got into the back corridor while we had the loading dock doors open. It must have just flown right in from the alley. And ever since that exact moment, for the last two years... man, it’s just been like a switch flipped in my brain. It's like there's a clear dividing line in my life. Before that shift, I was completely fine. But ever since that night in the corridor, for the past two years, it's just been this massive, overwhelming issue for me. So yeah, before that incident? Nothing. No bad experiences at all.

I know the statistics, I know they're more scared of us than we are of them, all that stuff people always tell you. \n\nBut if I see anything that even remotely looks like one, or if there's even just a quick flutter in the back hallway by the loading dock where the fluorescent lighting is always kind of flickering and terrible... my mind just completely treats it like I'm in real danger. Like, actual, immediate, life-or-death danger. \n\nIt's like a switch flips and all that logic about it just being a harmless little critter goes straight out the window. My brain doesn't say, oh hey, there's a bat, let's go tell Greg and call animal control. My brain just screams at me that I am in imminent peril and I need to survive. It's so frustrating because I can tell myself all day long that it's fine, but the absolute second I see that specific shape or that weird, frantic wing movement in the shadows, my body just reacts like I'm staring down a lion or something. It's just this total, overwhelming sense of danger that I completely cannot talk myself out of, no matter how hard I try to be rational about it.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything about other flying animals, such as their movement or appearance, that triggers a similar fear reaction in you?\n\nPatient: No, no, not at all. Birds don't bother me one bit. It's funny you ask, actually, because my neighbor—Mrs. Higgins, she lives two houses down from me—she has this massive bird feeder setup in her backyard. I mean, it's like a whole avian buffet out there. She's got the suet cakes, the black oil sunflower seeds, the thistle feeders for the finches... the whole nine yards. And my bedroom window looks right out over her yard. So, especially in the mornings, I see birds swooping around all the time. Cardinals, blue jays, chickadees, you name it. They're constantly darting in and out, fluttering around, sometimes they even chase each other right past my window. \n\nAnd it doesn't phase me in the slightest. I actually kind of like watching them while I drink my coffee before I head into the store. Their movement is just... different. It's graceful, you know? Even when they're flying fast or changing direction quickly, it makes sense to my brain. It's a bird doing bird things. It's not that erratic, jerky, unpredictable flapping that bats do. \n\nAnd as for bugs? Like, flying insects? Man, working in the garden center sometimes during the summer, you get all sorts of things flying at your face. We get these huge moths sometimes, especially around the outdoor floodlights when we're closing up at night. And yeah, they flutter, and they can be kind of gross when they dive-bomb your forehead, but it's just annoying. I just swat them away. Same with bees or wasps. If a wasp flies near me, I might take a step back because I don't want to get stung, but it's a totally rational reaction. It's not that cold, absolute panic. \n\nI've even had a bird get stuck in the store before! Last spring, a sparrow somehow got through the automatic doors up front and ended up flying all the way back to the hardware aisles. It was fluttering around up near the ceiling, clearly panicked, trying to find a way out. And everyone else was kind of ducking and pointing at it, but I wasn't scared at all. I actually felt bad for the poor thing. Greg and I spent like twenty minutes trying to herd it back toward the front doors with a couple of pool noodles we grabbed from seasonal. It was swooping all over the place, sometimes coming pretty close to our heads, but my heart rate didn't even go up. I was just focused on helping it get outside. \n\nSo, yeah, it's really not just the flying aspect, or the fluttering, or things moving fast in the air. If it's a bird, a moth, a butterfly, whatever... totally fine. It is exclusively, one hundred percent, just the bats. There's just something so specific about the way they look and the way they move that my brain just absolutely cannot handle. Everything else with wings gets a total pass.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any negative experiences or associations with bats before the incident in the stockroom two years ago?\n\nPatient: You know, thinking back on it... no. Not at all. And I guess that’s what’s so crazy about all of this. Before that, I never really thought about bats one way or another. I mean, maybe I saw them on nature documentaries, or flying way up high against the sky when I was a kid playing outside in the summer, but they never bothered me. They were just... there, you know? Completely off my radar. \n\nBut this whole thing, this intense reaction I have now? It’s really only been going on for about two years. It all traces back exactly to that one specific night. I was working a late receiving shift—which, usually, those are pretty quiet, routine shifts. The delivery trucks usually roll up around eight or nine at night, and we just spend a few hours breaking down pallets in the back corridor before we haul the stock out to the main floor. It's usually just me, maybe one other guy, listening to the radio and cutting open cardboard boxes. \n\nBut that night, that one specific receiving shift two years ago, that bat somehow got into the back corridor while we had the loading dock doors open. It must have just flown right in from the alley. And ever since that exact moment, for the last two years... man, it’s just been like a switch flipped in my brain. It's like there's a clear dividing line in my life. Before that shift, I was completely fine. But ever since that night in the corridor, for the past two years, it's just been this massive, overwhelming issue for me. So yeah, before that incident? Nothing. No bad experiences at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, honestly... no. It's just the bats. Which is, I guess, kind of the weirdest part of all this, you know? Because I know a lot of people have, like, a whole list of things that freak them out, but for me, it is literally just this one single thing. 

I mean, my sister, for instance, she absolutely cannot handle spiders. If there's even just a little daddy longlegs up in the corner of her ceiling, she's practically hyperventilating. She'll call her husband at work just to come home and get it with a cup. But me? If I see a spider in the garden center, even one of those big ugly wolf spiders, I just grab a paper towel and squish it, or I scoop it up and toss it outside. Doesn't make my heart rate go up a single beat. 

And it's the same with, like, heights or tight spaces. People always talk about being terrified of heights, but last autumn, we had this massive storm that blew a ton of leaves from the oak trees all over the store's roof, completely clogged up the drainage system. Greg and I had to take the extension ladder all the way up there to clear the gutters. I was standing right on the edge, two stories up, looking straight down at the concrete parking lot, and my stomach didn't even drop. No vertigo, no sweaty palms, nothing. I was just annoyed that it was raining and cold. 

I've even had to crawl into the crawlspace under my own back porch to fix a broken pipe a few years ago. It was pitch black down there, dirt everywhere, completely cramped—I had to sort of army-crawl on my elbows just to reach the shutoff valve—and I was totally fine. I was just mad that I was ruining a good pair of jeans. I get my flu shot every year at the pharmacy next door without batting an eye, I'm fine with snakes, I'm fine with dogs... 

So, yeah, it's really... it's exclusively just the bats. If it's not a bat, I'm just a completely normal, rational guy. It's so frustrating because I look at how I handle literally every other potentially scary thing in life, and I'm totally cool, calm, and collected. But then you throw a bat into the mix, and it's like my brain just completely short-circuits. But yeah, to answer your question, no. There is absolutely nothing else in the world that makes me feel that way. Just them.
